Well, honestly, I'm just... I'm really struggling right now, and I didn't know what else to do. I manage a busy administrative office, and you know, the days are just so long and stressful, dealing with everyone's paperwork and complaints all day. By the time I leave work, I'm just exhausted, completely drained. And lately, it's just gotten so out of hand. I came in because I keep having these evenings... it's almost like a routine now, which is terrifying to admit. I'll stop on the way home and buy a whole family-size takeout order—like, enough food for four people. And then, as if that isn't enough, I'll stop at the grocery store and grab bags of chips, or maybe some pastries from the bakery section. I tell myself I'm just getting a little treat for the week, but then I get home, and I sit down, and... I just start eating. And I eat, and I eat, and before I even seem to register how much is actually gone, I've eaten almost all of it myself. It's like I just blank out or something, and then I'm sitting there with all these empty containers, feeling so sick and so ashamed. I just... I can't seem to stop doing it, and it's really starting to scare me.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
How do I feel? Oh, it's... it's just this overwhelming wave of absolute disgust with myself. I mean, my stomach physically hurts, of course, it feels so stretched and heavy, like I've just swallowed a boulder, but the emotional part is so, so much worse. 

I'll be sitting there on my couch—it's this older beige sofa I've had for years, right in front of the TV—and I just stare at the coffee table. It'll be completely covered in all these greasy wrappers and plastic bags and empty cartons, and the guilt is just paralyzing. I start calculating how much money I just wasted on all of it, and I think, 'Why did I do this again?' Because I promise myself every single morning that I won't. I'll pack a sensible lunch for work, you know, maybe a nice salad or a turkey sandwich, and I'll tell myself today is the day I go home and just have a normal, regular-sized dinner. I'm a supervisor, for heaven's sake! I manage a whole team of administrative staff, I handle complex schedules and budgets all day long... I should be able to manage my own evening routine. 

But then I do it anyway, and afterward, sitting there in the mess... I just cry sometimes. I feel so completely out of control, like I'm just a spectator in my own life watching this woman ruin herself. It's so humiliating, honestly. I actually find myself panicking and rushing to gather all the bags and cartons, and I'll take them out in the dark and shove them deep down into the outside trash bin so no one sees. I live alone, but I'm terrified the neighbors or even the garbage collectors will see all those family-sized takeout containers and judge me, if you can believe that. It’s just this heavy, dark cloud of shame that settles over me, and it doesn't really lift. I just sit there feeling completely defeated, dreading having to wake up and do the whole cycle over again the next day.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, no, I've never... I don't do anything like that. I know some people do, you know, you see it on TV or read about it in magazines, where people try to undo it by throwing up or taking pills, but I've never been able to bring myself to do that. I actually have a terrible fear of throwing up, always have, ever since I was a little girl and had this awful bout of the stomach flu that lasted for three days. My mother had to stay home from work to take care of me, and I just remember feeling so utterly helpless and miserable. So, no, the thought of making myself sick on purpose... it just repulses me, honestly. 

And as for laxatives, well, I tried them once years ago when I was actually just having some normal digestive issues, and the cramping was so horrendous I swore I'd never touch them again. I remember I was at work, trying to run a staff meeting about the new filing system, and I had to keep excusing myself. It was so embarrassing. 

So, no, I just... I just sit there with it. I sit there with all that food inside me, feeling so incredibly full and bloated and uncomfortable. Sometimes I'll try to drink some peppermint tea or maybe lay on my left side, because I read somewhere once that laying on your left side helps with digestion, but mostly I just suffer through it. I just let it sit there like a rock, a heavy, awful rock, and I just deal with the physical discomfort on top of all the emotional misery I was telling you about. It's like I feel I deserve the physical pain, you know? Like it's my punishment for losing control again. So, no, I don't try to get rid of it. I just let it stay there and make me miserable until it finally passes on its own, which usually takes well into the next day.

By the time I leave work, I'm just exhausted, completely drained. And lately, it's just gotten so out of hand. I came in because I keep having these evenings... it's almost like a routine now, which is terrifying to admit. I'll stop on the way home and buy a whole family-size takeout order—like, enough food for four people. It'll be completely covered in all these greasy wrappers and plastic bags and empty cartons, and the guilt is just paralyzing. I start calculating how much money I just wasted on all of it, and I think, 'Why did I do this again?' Because I promise myself every single morning that I won't. I actually have a terrible fear of throwing up, always have, ever since I was a little girl and had this awful bout of the stomach flu that lasted for three days. My mother had to stay home from work to take care of me, and I just remember feeling so utterly helpless and miserable. So, no, the thought of making myself sick on purpose... it just repulses me, honestly. \n\nAnd as for laxatives, well, I tried them once years ago when I was actually just having some normal digestive issues, and the cramping was so horrendous I swore I'd never touch them again. I remember I was at work, trying to run a staff meeting about the new filing system, and I had to keep excusing myself. It was so embarrassing. \n\nSo, no, I just... I just sit there with it. I sit there with all that food inside me, feeling so incredibly full and bloated and uncomfortable. Sometimes I'll try to drink some peppermint tea or maybe lay on my left side, because I read somewhere once that laying on your left side helps with digestion, but mostly I just suffer through it. I just let it sit there like a rock, a heavy, awful rock, and I just deal with the physical discomfort on top of all the emotional misery I was telling you about. It's like I feel I deserve the physical pain, you know? Like it's my punishment for losing control again. So, no, I don't try to get rid of it. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, it's... it's a lot. More than I want to admit, honestly. I actually keep a journal—have for years, it's this little leather-bound thing I keep in my nightstand. I started it just to track my daily tasks at work, you know, because keeping track of all the administrative staff's schedules and the endless office supplies orders can get so overwhelming, but lately it's turned into this awful log of my failures. 

When I look back through it, just flipping through the pages from the last few months... it's really distressing. There has barely been a single week in the last several months without at least one of these nights. And I mean barely. Most weeks... most weeks it happens several times. Like, I'll do it on a Tuesday, and then I'll be so angry with myself on Wednesday that I manage to hold it together, but by Thursday the stress of the office is back up to my neck—maybe someone called out sick or the payroll system glitched—and I'm right back at the drive-thru window. I'll order the food, pull into my driveway, and the whole cycle starts again. 

Sometimes it's three or four times a week. I try to write down what triggered it, thinking maybe if I can just pinpoint the exact moment my day went wrong, I could fix it. Like, 'Oh, Jane from accounting yelled at me about a missing invoice at 2 PM, so that's why I bought two large pizzas at 6 PM.' But it doesn't even seem to matter anymore. It's just happening all the time, week after week, month after month. It's like my whole life outside of the office has just become about when the next time is going to happen, and I'm just so exhausted by it.

I started it just to track my daily tasks at work, you know, because keeping track of all the administrative staff's schedules and the endless office supplies orders can get so overwhelming, but lately it's turned into this awful log of my failures. \n\nWhen I look back through it, just flipping through the pages from the last few months... it's really distressing. There has barely been a single week in the last several months without at least one of these nights. And I mean barely. Most weeks... most weeks it happens several times. Like, I'll do it on a Tuesday, and then I'll be so angry with myself on Wednesday that I manage to hold it together, but by Thursday the stress of the office is back up to my neck—maybe someone called out sick or the payroll system glitched—and I'm right back at the drive-thru window. I'll order the food, pull into my driveway, and the whole cycle starts again. \n\nSometimes it's three or four times a week. I try to write down what triggered it, thinking maybe if I can just pinpoint the exact moment my day went wrong, I could fix it. Like, 'Oh, Jane from accounting yelled at me about a missing invoice at 2 PM, so that's why I bought two large pizzas at 6 PM.' But it doesn't even seem to matter anymore. It's just happening all the time, week after week, month after month. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Guilty after a normal meal? Oh, no, not really. I mean... well, it's different. A normal meal, like... like I mentioned before, if I pack a salad for lunch or have a turkey sandwich at my desk while I'm reviewing the weekly timesheets—because my team has to have their hours submitted by noon on Thursdays or payroll gets entirely backed up, and you wouldn't believe the headache that causes—when I eat something like that, I actually feel good about it. I feel proud, almost. Like I'm finally doing what I'm supposed to be doing. I'll sit there with my little tupperware container and think, 'See? You can be a normal person who eats a normal amount of food.' 

It's only when the switch flips, you know? When I lose that control. If I go out to a restaurant with a friend—not that I do that very often anymore, because I'm so self-conscious about eating in front of people, but if I do—and I just order a regular entree, maybe a piece of grilled chicken and some vegetables, I don't feel that crushing guilt afterward. I might feel a little anxious beforehand, worrying about what the menu will look like or if I'll be tempted to order the whole appetizer platter, but the meal itself... if I keep it together, I feel okay. 

The guilt is really tied entirely to the sheer volume of those bad nights. It's the physical evidence of it, the wrappers and the boxes, and the memory of just shoveling it in without even tasting it. That's where the shame comes from. A regular dinner, if I can manage it, actually feels like a tiny little victory, even if it's just for one day. It's just that those victories are getting so incredibly rare lately.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n5\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, honestly, I'm just... I'm really struggling right now, and I didn't know what else to do. I manage a busy administrative office, and you know, the days are just so long and stressful, dealing with everyone's paperwork and complaints all day. By the time I leave work, I'm just exhausted, completely drained. And lately, it's just gotten so out of hand. I came in because I keep having these evenings... it's almost like a routine now, which is terrifying to admit. I'll stop on the way home and buy a whole family-size takeout order—like, enough food for four people. And then, as if that isn't enough, I'll stop at the grocery store and grab bags of chips, or maybe some pastries from the bakery section. I tell myself I'm just getting a little treat for the week, but then I get home, and I sit down, and... I just start eating. And I eat, and I eat, and before I even seem to register how much is actually gone, I've eaten almost all of it myself. It's like I just blank out or something, and then I'm sitting there with all these empty containers, feeling so sick and so ashamed. I just... I can't seem to stop doing it, and it's really starting to scare me.\n\nDoctor: How do you feel after you've eaten all that food and you're sitting there with the empty containers?\n\nPatient: How do I feel? Oh, it's... it's just this overwhelming wave of absolute disgust with myself. I mean, my stomach physically hurts, of course, it feels so stretched and heavy, like I've just swallowed a boulder, but the emotional part is so, so much worse. \n\nI'll be sitting there on my couch—it's this older beige sofa I've had for years, right in front of the TV—and I just stare at the coffee table. It'll be completely covered in all these greasy wrappers and plastic bags and empty cartons, and the guilt is just paralyzing. I start calculating how much money I just wasted on all of it, and I think, 'Why did I do this again?' Because I promise myself every single morning that I won't. I'll pack a sensible lunch for work, you know, maybe a nice salad or a turkey sandwich, and I'll tell myself today is the day I go home and just have a normal, regular-sized dinner. I'm a supervisor, for heaven's sake! I manage a whole team of administrative staff, I handle complex schedules and budgets all day long... I should be able to manage my own evening routine. \n\nBut then I do it anyway, and afterward, sitting there in the mess... I just cry sometimes. I feel so completely out of control, like I'm just a spectator in my own life watching this woman ruin herself. It's so humiliating, honestly. I actually find myself panicking and rushing to gather all the bags and cartons, and I'll take them out in the dark and shove them deep down into the outside trash bin so no one sees. I live alone, but I'm terrified the neighbors or even the garbage collectors will see all those family-sized takeout containers and judge me, if you can believe that. It’s just this heavy, dark cloud of shame that settles over me, and it doesn't really lift. I just sit there feeling completely defeated, dreading having to wake up and do the whole cycle over again the next day.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit or use laxatives after one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: No, no, I've never... I don't do anything like that. I know some people do, you know, you see it on TV or read about it in magazines, where people try to undo it by throwing up or taking pills, but I've never been able to bring myself to do that. I actually have a terrible fear of throwing up, always have, ever since I was a little girl and had this awful bout of the stomach flu that lasted for three days. My mother had to stay home from work to take care of me, and I just remember feeling so utterly helpless and miserable. So, no, the thought of making myself sick on purpose... it just repulses me, honestly. \n\nAnd as for laxatives, well, I tried them once years ago when I was actually just having some normal digestive issues, and the cramping was so horrendous I swore I'd never touch them again. I remember I was at work, trying to run a staff meeting about the new filing system, and I had to keep excusing myself. It was so embarrassing. \n\nSo, no, I just... I just sit there with it. I sit there with all that food inside me, feeling so incredibly full and bloated and uncomfortable. Sometimes I'll try to drink some peppermint tea or maybe lay on my left side, because I read somewhere once that laying on your left side helps with digestion, but mostly I just suffer through it. I just let it sit there like a rock, a heavy, awful rock, and I just deal with the physical discomfort on top of all the emotional misery I was telling you about. It's like I feel I deserve the physical pain, you know? Like it's my punishment for losing control again. So, no, I don't try to get rid of it. I just let it stay there and make me miserable until it finally passes on its own, which usually takes well into the next day.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating a large amount of food in a short time happen?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... it's a lot. More than I want to admit, honestly. I actually keep a journal—have for years, it's this little leather-bound thing I keep in my nightstand. I started it just to track my daily tasks at work, you know, because keeping track of all the administrative staff's schedules and the endless office supplies orders can get so overwhelming, but lately it's turned into this awful log of my failures. \n\nWhen I look back through it, just flipping through the pages from the last few months... it's really distressing. There has barely been a single week in the last several months without at least one of these nights. And I mean barely. Most weeks... most weeks it happens several times. Like, I'll do it on a Tuesday, and then I'll be so angry with myself on Wednesday that I manage to hold it together, but by Thursday the stress of the office is back up to my neck—maybe someone called out sick or the payroll system glitched—and I'm right back at the drive-thru window. I'll order the food, pull into my driveway, and the whole cycle starts again. \n\nSometimes it's three or four times a week. I try to write down what triggered it, thinking maybe if I can just pinpoint the exact moment my day went wrong, I could fix it. Like, 'Oh, Jane from accounting yelled at me about a missing invoice at 2 PM, so that's why I bought two large pizzas at 6 PM.' But it doesn't even seem to matter anymore. It's just happening all the time, week after week, month after month. It's like my whole life outside of the office has just become about when the next time is going to happen, and I'm just so exhausted by it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty after eating a normal meal?\n\nPatient: Guilty after a normal meal? Oh, no, not really. I mean... well, it's different. A normal meal, like... like I mentioned before, if I pack a salad for lunch or have a turkey sandwich at my desk while I'm reviewing the weekly timesheets—because my team has to have their hours submitted by noon on Thursdays or payroll gets entirely backed up, and you wouldn't believe the headache that causes—when I eat something like that, I actually feel good about it. I feel proud, almost. Like I'm finally doing what I'm supposed to be doing. I'll sit there with my little tupperware container and think, 'See? You can be a normal person who eats a normal amount of food.' \n\nIt's only when the switch flips, you know? When I lose that control. If I go out to a restaurant with a friend—not that I do that very often anymore, because I'm so self-conscious about eating in front of people, but if I do—and I just order a regular entree, maybe a piece of grilled chicken and some vegetables, I don't feel that crushing guilt afterward. I might feel a little anxious beforehand, worrying about what the menu will look like or if I'll be tempted to order the whole appetizer platter, but the meal itself... if I keep it together, I feel okay. \n\nThe guilt is really tied entirely to the sheer volume of those bad nights. It's the physical evidence of it, the wrappers and the boxes, and the memory of just shoveling it in without even tasting it. That's where the shame comes from. A regular dinner, if I can manage it, actually feels like a tiny little victory, even if it's just for one day. It's just that those victories are getting so incredibly rare lately.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

Sometimes I'll try to drink some peppermint tea or maybe lay on my left side, because I read somewhere once that laying on your left side helps with digestion, but mostly I just suffer through it. I just let it sit there like a rock, a heavy, awful rock, and I just deal with the physical discomfort on top of all the emotional misery I was telling you about. It's like I feel I deserve the physical pain, you know? Like it's my punishment for losing control again. So, no, I don't try to get rid of it. I just let it stay there and make me miserable until it finally passes on its own, which usually takes well into the next day.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating a large amount of food in a short time happen?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... it's a lot. More than I want to admit, honestly. I actually keep a journal—have for years, it's this little leather-bound thing I keep in my nightstand. I started it just to track my daily tasks at work, you know, because keeping track of all the administrative staff's schedules and the endless office supplies orders can get so overwhelming, but lately it's turned into this awful log of my failures. \n\nWhen I look back through it, just flipping through the pages from the last few months... it's really distressing. There has barely been a single week in the last several months without at least one of these nights. And I mean barely. Most weeks... most weeks it happens several times. Like, I'll do it on a Tuesday, and then I'll be so angry with myself on Wednesday that I manage to hold it together, but by Thursday the stress of the office is back up to my neck—maybe someone called out sick or the payroll system glitched—and I'm right back at the drive-thru window. I'll order the food, pull into my driveway, and the whole cycle starts again. \n\nSometimes it's three or four times a week. I try to write down what triggered it, thinking maybe if I can just pinpoint the exact moment my day went wrong, I could fix it. Like, 'Oh, Jane from accounting yelled at me about a missing invoice at 2 PM, so that's why I bought two large pizzas at 6 PM.' But it doesn't even seem to matter anymore. It's just happening all the time, week after week, month after month. It's like my whole life outside of the office has just become about when the next time is going to happen, and I'm just so exhausted by it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty after eating a normal meal?\n\nPatient: Guilty after a normal meal? Oh, no, not really. I mean... well, it's different. A normal meal, like... like I mentioned before, if I pack a salad for lunch or have a turkey sandwich at my desk while I'm reviewing the weekly timesheets—because my team has to have their hours submitted by noon on Thursdays or payroll gets entirely backed up, and you wouldn't believe the headache that causes—when I eat something like that, I actually feel good about it. I feel proud, almost. Like I'm finally doing what I'm supposed to be doing. I'll sit there with my little tupperware container and think, 'See? You can be a normal person who eats a normal amount of food.' \n\nIt's only when the switch flips, you know? When I lose that control. If I go out to a restaurant with a friend—not that I do that very often anymore, because I'm so self-conscious about eating in front of people, but if I do—and I just order a regular entree, maybe a piece of grilled chicken and some vegetables, I don't feel that crushing guilt afterward. I might feel a little anxious beforehand, worrying about what the menu will look like or if I'll be tempted to order the whole appetizer platter, but the meal itself... if I keep it together, I feel okay. \n\nThe guilt is really tied entirely to the sheer volume of those bad nights. It's the physical evidence of it, the wrappers and the boxes, and the memory of just shoveling it in without even tasting it. That's where the shame comes from. A regular dinner, if I can manage it, actually feels like a tiny little victory, even if it's just for one day. It's just that those victories are getting so incredibly rare lately.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you usually feel stressed, anxious, or upset right before one of these episodes of eating a large amount of food?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 54 / first_line_supervisor_of_office_or_administrative_support_worker / associates\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well... yes and no. I mean, it's not always this huge, dramatic thing right beforehand. Sometimes it is, of course. Like I was saying about the office—if someone calls out sick and I have to cover their desk on top of managing the whole administrative team, or if the quarterly reports are due and the system crashes... yes, I feel this intense, buzzing sort of stress right behind my eyes. I'll be sitting at my desk, my heart will be racing, and I can almost feel this tightness in my chest, and all I can think about is getting out of there and getting to the food. It's like a pressure valve that needs to be released. In those moments, I am absolutely, undeniably stressed and upset. 

But then... there are other times. Times where I wouldn't necessarily say I'm feeling actively upset or anxious right in that exact moment. Like, it might just be a regular Tuesday evening. I've left the office, the drive home was fine, traffic wasn't even that bad—which is a miracle on the interstate these days—and I'll pull into my driveway, and it's just... quiet. The house is quiet. And it's almost like the absence of stress becomes its own trigger, if that makes sense? It's like this creeping emptiness or boredom, or maybe it's just habit at this point. I'll walk through the front door, put my keys on the little table in the hall, and before I've even taken off my coat, I'm already thinking about what I can order or what's in the pantry. 

So, I guess what I'm trying to say is, I don't always feel this acute, sharp anxiety right before I start eating. Sometimes I do, absolutely, especially when work is chaotic. But other times, it's more like this dull, heavy feeling... like a fog that just rolls in, and the only way I know how to navigate through it is by eating. It's almost like my brain just goes on autopilot, regardless of whether I'm having a terrible day or just a remarkably average one. Does that make sense? It's just... it's not always a clear-cut reaction to a specific upset, even though I know that's probably what you're looking for. It's just so ingrained in my routine now.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, honestly, I'm just... I'm really struggling right now, and I didn't know what else to do. By the time I leave work, I'm just exhausted, completely drained. And lately, it's just gotten so out of hand. It'll be completely covered in all these greasy wrappers and plastic bags and empty cartons, and the guilt is just paralyzing. I don't do anything like that. I actually have a terrible fear of throwing up, always have, ever since I was a little girl and had this awful bout of the stomach flu that lasted for three days. My mother had to stay home from work to take care of me, and I just remember feeling so utterly helpless and miserable. So, no, the thought of making myself sick on purpose... it just repulses me, honestly. \n\nAnd as for laxatives, well, I tried them once years ago when I was actually just having some normal digestive issues, and the cramping was so horrendous I swore I'd never touch them again. I remember I was at work, trying to run a staff meeting about the new filing system, and I had to keep excusing myself. It was so embarrassing. \n\nSo, no, I just... I just sit there with it. Well, I mean... I've really, really tried to figure that exact thing out. I actually—and this might sound a little silly—but I actually tried to start color-coding that journal I told you about. Because, you know, in my line of work, if you want to solve a problem with the administrative workflow, you track the metrics. You find the root cause. So I thought, okay, let's track my emotions. I bought these little highlighters, thinking I'd use yellow for anxiety, blue for sadness, maybe pink for anger... just to see if a pattern emerged before I ended up with all those food wrappers. 

But... I honestly don't know. I really couldn't tell you if it's one specific thing like sadness or anxiety. It's just not that clear-cut. I mean, sure, sometimes I think I'm sad. Like, my daughter moved a few states away a couple of years ago, and that was terribly sad, but the nights I missed her the absolute most weren't necessarily the nights I lost control with food. And anxiety... well, like I said before about the payroll glitches and the office, that definitely revs me up. 

But then there are those other days. I'll just be sitting in my living room on a Sunday afternoon, watching one of those home renovation shows on TV, not feeling sad, not feeling anxious, not feeling much of anything at all, really. Just perfectly neutral. And then, out of nowhere, it's like a switch flips and I'm ordering forty dollars worth of Chinese takeout. 

So, I guess... no, I don't think it's usually triggered by a specific emotional state. Or if it is, I certainly haven't been able to identify it. It's so incredibly frustrating, because if I could just say, 'Oh, I eat when I'm sad,' then maybe I could just... I don't know, call a friend or watch a comedy when I feel the sadness coming on. But because it happens when I'm anxious, and when I'm stressed, and when I'm completely fine and just folding laundry... I just don't have a clear answer for you. It's just this big, confusing jumble, and the highlighters didn't help at all.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, honestly, I'm just... I'm really struggling right now, and I didn't know what else to do. By the time I leave work, I'm just exhausted, completely drained. And lately, it's just gotten so out of hand. I'll stop on the way home and buy a whole family-size takeout order—like, enough food for four people. And then, as if that isn't enough, I'll stop at the grocery store and grab bags of chips, or maybe some pastries from the bakery section. It's like I just blank out or something, and then I'm sitting there with all these empty containers, feeling so sick and so ashamed. I just... I can't seem to stop doing it, and it's really starting to scare me.\n\nDoctor: How do you feel after you've eaten all that food and you're sitting there with the empty containers?\n\nPatient: How do I feel? Oh, it's... it's just this overwhelming wave of absolute disgust with myself. It'll be completely covered in all these greasy wrappers and plastic bags and empty cartons, and the guilt is just paralyzing. I start calculating how much money I just wasted on all of it, and I think, 'Why did I do this again?' Because I promise myself every single morning that I won't. I'll pack a sensible lunch for work, you know, maybe a nice salad or a turkey sandwich, and I'll tell myself today is the day I go home and just have a normal, regular-sized dinner. I should be able to manage my own evening routine. \n\nBut then I do it anyway, and afterward, sitting there in the mess... I just cry sometimes. I feel so completely out of control, like I'm just a spectator in my own life watching this woman ruin herself. It's so humiliating, honestly. It’s just this heavy, dark cloud of shame that settles over me, and it doesn't really lift. I actually have a terrible fear of throwing up, always have, ever since I was a little girl and had this awful bout of the stomach flu that lasted for three days. My mother had to stay home from work to take care of me, and I just remember feeling so utterly helpless and miserable. So, no, the thought of making myself sick on purpose... it just repulses me, honestly. \n\nAnd as for laxatives, well, I tried them once years ago when I was actually just having some normal digestive issues, and the cramping was so horrendous I swore I'd never touch them again. I remember I was at work, trying to run a staff meeting about the new filing system, and I had to keep excusing myself. It was so embarrassing. \n\nSo, no, I just... I just sit there with it. I just let it sit there like a rock, a heavy, awful rock, and I just deal with the physical discomfort on top of all the emotional misery I was telling you about. I'll be sitting at my desk, my heart will be racing, and I can almost feel this tightness in my chest, and all I can think about is getting out of there and getting to the food. It's like a pressure valve that needs to be released. In those moments, I am absolutely, undeniably stressed and upset. \n\nBut then... there are other times. Times where I wouldn't necessarily say I'm feeling actively upset or anxious right in that exact moment. Like, it might just be a regular Tuesday evening. I've left the office, the drive home was fine, traffic wasn't even that bad—which is a miracle on the interstate these days—and I'll pull into my driveway, and it's just... quiet. The house is quiet. And it's almost like the absence of stress becomes its own trigger, if that makes sense? It's like this creeping emptiness or boredom, or maybe it's just habit at this point. I'll walk through the front door, put my keys on the little table in the hall, and before I've even taken off my coat, I'm already thinking about what I can order or what's in the pantry. \n\nSo, I guess what I'm trying to say is, I don't always feel this acute, sharp anxiety right before I start eating. Sometimes I do, absolutely, especially when work is chaotic. But other times, it's more like this dull, heavy feeling... like a fog that just rolls in, and the only way I know how to navigate through it is by eating. It's almost like my brain just goes on autopilot, regardless of whether I'm having a terrible day or just a remarkably average one. Does that make sense? It's just... it's not always a clear-cut reaction to a specific upset, even though I know that's probably what you're looking for. It's just so ingrained in my routine now.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating episodes are usually triggered by a specific emotional state, such as sadness or anxiety?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean... I've really, really tried to figure that exact thing out. I actually—and this might sound a little silly—but I actually tried to start color-coding that journal I told you about. I bought these little highlighters, thinking I'd use yellow for anxiety, blue for sadness, maybe pink for anger... just to see if a pattern emerged before I ended up with all those food wrappers. \n\nBut... I honestly don't know. I really couldn't tell you if it's one specific thing like sadness or anxiety. It's just not that clear-cut. I mean, sure, sometimes I think I'm sad. I'll just be sitting in my living room on a Sunday afternoon, watching one of those home renovation shows on TV, not feeling sad, not feeling anxious, not feeling much of anything at all, really. Just perfectly neutral. And then, out of nowhere, it's like a switch flips and I'm ordering forty dollars worth of Chinese takeout. \n\nSo, I guess... no, I don't think it's usually triggered by a specific emotional state. If I go out to a restaurant with a friend—not that I do that very often anymore, because I'm so self-conscious about eating in front of people, but if I do—and I just order a regular entree, maybe a piece of grilled chicken and some vegetables, I don't feel that crushing guilt afterward. I might feel a little anxious beforehand, worrying about what the menu will look like or if I'll be tempted to order the whole appetizer platter, but the meal itself... if I keep it together, I feel okay. \n\nThe guilt is really tied entirely to the sheer volume of those bad nights. It's the physical evidence of it, the wrappers and the boxes, and the memory of just shoveling it in without even tasting it. That's where the shame comes from. A regular dinner, if I can manage it, actually feels like a tiny little victory, even if it's just for one day. It's just that those victories are getting so incredibly rare lately.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel stressed, anxious, or upset right before one of these episodes of eating a large amount of food?\n\nPatient: Well... yes and no. I mean, it's not always this huge, dramatic thing right beforehand. Sometimes it is, of course. Like I was saying about the office—if someone calls out sick and I have to cover their desk on top of managing the whole administrative team, or if the quarterly reports are due and the system crashes... yes, I feel this intense, buzzing sort of stress right behind my eyes. I'll be sitting at my desk, my heart will be racing, and I can almost feel this tightness in my chest, and all I can think about is getting out of there and getting to the food. It's like a pressure valve that needs to be released. In those moments, I am absolutely, undeniably stressed and upset. \n\nBut then... there are other times. Times where I wouldn't necessarily say I'm feeling actively upset or anxious right in that exact moment. Like, it might just be a regular Tuesday evening. I've left the office, the drive home was fine, traffic wasn't even that bad—which is a miracle on the interstate these days—and I'll pull into my driveway, and it's just... quiet. The house is quiet. And it's almost like the absence of stress becomes its own trigger, if that makes sense? It's like this creeping emptiness or boredom, or maybe it's just habit at this point. I'll walk through the front door, put my keys on the little table in the hall, and before I've even taken off my coat, I'm already thinking about what I can order or what's in the pantry. \n\nSo, I guess what I'm trying to say is, I don't always feel this acute, sharp anxiety right before I start eating. Sometimes I do, absolutely, especially when work is chaotic. But other times, it's more like this dull, heavy feeling... like a fog that just rolls in, and the only way I know how to navigate through it is by eating. It's almost like my brain just goes on autopilot, regardless of whether I'm having a terrible day or just a remarkably average one. Does that make sense? It's just... it's not always a clear-cut reaction to a specific upset, even though I know that's probably what you're looking for. It's just so ingrained in my routine now.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating episodes are usually triggered by a specific emotional state, such as sadness or anxiety?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean... I've really, really tried to figure that exact thing out. I actually—and this might sound a little silly—but I actually tried to start color-coding that journal I told you about. Because, you know, in my line of work, if you want to solve a problem with the administrative workflow, you track the metrics. You find the root cause. So I thought, okay, let's track my emotions. I bought these little highlighters, thinking I'd use yellow for anxiety, blue for sadness, maybe pink for anger... just to see if a pattern emerged before I ended up with all those food wrappers. \n\nBut... I honestly don't know. I really couldn't tell you if it's one specific thing like sadness or anxiety. It's just not that clear-cut. I mean, sure, sometimes I think I'm sad. Like, my daughter moved a few states away a couple of years ago, and that was terribly sad, but the nights I missed her the absolute most weren't necessarily the nights I lost control with food. And anxiety... well, like I said before about the payroll glitches and the office, that definitely revs me up. \n\nBut then there are those other days. I'll just be sitting in my living room on a Sunday afternoon, watching one of those home renovation shows on TV, not feeling sad, not feeling anxious, not feeling much of anything at all, really. Just perfectly neutral. And then, out of nowhere, it's like a switch flips and I'm ordering forty dollars worth of Chinese takeout. \n\nSo, I guess... no, I don't think it's usually triggered by a specific emotional state. Or if it is, I certainly haven't been able to identify it. It's so incredibly frustrating, because if I could just say, 'Oh, I eat when I'm sad,' then maybe I could just... I don't know, call a friend or watch a comedy when I feel the sadness coming on. But because it happens when I'm anxious, and when I'm stressed, and when I'm completely fine and just folding laundry... I just don't have a clear answer for you. It's just this big, confusing jumble, and the highlighters didn't help at all.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel like you have control over what you're eating or the amount you're eating during these episodes?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 54 / first_line_supervisor_of_office_or_administrative_support_worker / associates\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Loss_of_Control\nmanifestation: \"Once I start, there is this awful switch that flips where I can be thinking, Taylor, put it away, and my hands still keep opening containers like I am not the one in charge.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Control? Oh, heavens, no. No, absolutely not. That is... honestly, Doctor, that is the most terrifying part of this whole ordeal. If I had control, I wouldn't be sitting here, you know? Because in literally every other aspect of my life, I am the one in control. I manage the whole front office, I tell people where to be and what to do, I fix the schedules when they completely fall apart, I handle the disputes... I am in charge. But with this? 

It's like... well, I mentioned that switch flipping earlier, but during the actual episode, it's so much worse than just a general feeling. It's a complete disconnect. Once I start, there is this awful switch that flips where I am just... severed from my own body, almost. I can literally be standing there at the kitchen counter, looking at all the food I've laid out, and in my head, my rational brain is still there. I can be thinking, Taylor, put it away. Just close the box, put it in the fridge, walk away. I hear it so clearly in my mind, just begging myself to stop. 

But my hands... they just keep moving. My hands still keep opening containers, ripping off wrappers, pulling out more food, like I am not the one in charge. It is the most bizarre, helpless experience you can imagine. It's like I'm a passenger in my own body, just watching my own hands do these things, and I'm pleading with myself to just put the fork down or close the cabinet door, but I just can't. The physical motion just completely overrides my brain. I'll just keep going and going, mechanically, until it's all gone, all while that voice inside is just screaming to stop. So, no, there is zero control. None whatsoever once that first bite happens. It's like I'm entirely hijacked.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, honestly, I'm just... I'm really struggling right now, and I didn't know what else to do. I manage a busy administrative office, and you know, the days are just so long and stressful, dealing with everyone's paperwork and complaints all day. By the time I leave work, I'm just exhausted, completely drained. And lately, it's just gotten so out of hand. I came in because I keep having these evenings... it's almost like a routine now, which is terrifying to admit. I'll stop on the way home and buy a whole family-size takeout order—like, enough food for four people. And then, as if that isn't enough, I'll stop at the grocery store and grab bags of chips, or maybe some pastries from the bakery section. I tell myself I'm just getting a little treat for the week, but then I get home, and I sit down, and... I just start eating. And I eat, and I eat, and before I even seem to register how much is actually gone, I've eaten almost all of it myself. It's like I just blank out or something, and then I'm sitting there with all these empty containers, feeling so sick and so ashamed. I just... I can't seem to stop doing it, and it's really starting to scare me.\n\nDoctor: How do you feel after you've eaten all that food and you're sitting there with the empty containers?\n\nPatient: How do I feel? Oh, it's... it's just this overwhelming wave of absolute disgust with myself. I mean, my stomach physically hurts, of course, it feels so stretched and heavy, like I've just swallowed a boulder, but the emotional part is so, so much worse. \n\nI'll be sitting there on my couch—it's this older beige sofa I've had for years, right in front of the TV—and I just stare at the coffee table. It'll be completely covered in all these greasy wrappers and plastic bags and empty cartons, and the guilt is just paralyzing. I start calculating how much money I just wasted on all of it, and I think, 'Why did I do this again?' Because I promise myself every single morning that I won't. I'll pack a sensible lunch for work, you know, maybe a nice salad or a turkey sandwich, and I'll tell myself today is the day I go home and just have a normal, regular-sized dinner. I'm a supervisor, for heaven's sake! I manage a whole team of administrative staff, I handle complex schedules and budgets all day long... I should be able to manage my own evening routine. \n\nBut then I do it anyway, and afterward, sitting there in the mess... I just cry sometimes. I feel so completely out of control, like I'm just a spectator in my own life watching this woman ruin herself. It's so humiliating, honestly. I actually find myself panicking and rushing to gather all the bags and cartons, and I'll take them out in the dark and shove them deep down into the outside trash bin so no one sees. I live alone, but I'm terrified the neighbors or even the garbage collectors will see all those family-sized takeout containers and judge me, if you can believe that. It’s just this heavy, dark cloud of shame that settles over me, and it doesn't really lift. I just sit there feeling completely defeated, dreading having to wake up and do the whole cycle over again the next day.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit or use laxatives after one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: No, no, I've never... I don't do anything like that. I know some people do, you know, you see it on TV or read about it in magazines, where people try to undo it by throwing up or taking pills, but I've never been able to bring myself to do that. I actually have a terrible fear of throwing up, always have, ever since I was a little girl and had this awful bout of the stomach flu that lasted for three days. My mother had to stay home from work to take care of me, and I just remember feeling so utterly helpless and miserable. So, no, the thought of making myself sick on purpose... it just repulses me, honestly. \n\nAnd as for laxatives, well, I tried them once years ago when I was actually just having some normal digestive issues, and the cramping was so horrendous I swore I'd never touch them again. I remember I was at work, trying to run a staff meeting about the new filing system, and I had to keep excusing myself. It was so embarrassing. \n\nSo, no, I just... I just sit there with it. I sit there with all that food inside me, feeling so incredibly full and bloated and uncomfortable. Sometimes I'll try to drink some peppermint tea or maybe lay on my left side, because I read somewhere once that laying on your left side helps with digestion, but mostly I just suffer through it. I just let it sit there like a rock, a heavy, awful rock, and I just deal with the physical discomfort on top of all the emotional misery I was telling you about. It's like I feel I deserve the physical pain, you know? Like it's my punishment for losing control again. So, no, I don't try to get rid of it. I just let it stay there and make me miserable until it finally passes on its own, which usually takes well into the next day.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating a large amount of food in a short time happen?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... it's a lot. More than I want to admit, honestly. I actually keep a journal—have for years, it's this little leather-bound thing I keep in my nightstand. I started it just to track my daily tasks at work, you know, because keeping track of all the administrative staff's schedules and the endless office supplies orders can get so overwhelming, but lately it's turned into this awful log of my failures. \n\nWhen I look back through it, just flipping through the pages from the last few months... it's really distressing. There has barely been a single week in the last several months without at least one of these nights. And I mean barely. Most weeks... most weeks it happens several times. Like, I'll do it on a Tuesday, and then I'll be so angry with myself on Wednesday that I manage to hold it together, but by Thursday the stress of the office is back up to my neck—maybe someone called out sick or the payroll system glitched—and I'm right back at the drive-thru window. I'll order the food, pull into my driveway, and the whole cycle starts again. \n\nSometimes it's three or four times a week. I try to write down what triggered it, thinking maybe if I can just pinpoint the exact moment my day went wrong, I could fix it. Like, 'Oh, Jane from accounting yelled at me about a missing invoice at 2 PM, so that's why I bought two large pizzas at 6 PM.' But it doesn't even seem to matter anymore. It's just happening all the time, week after week, month after month. It's like my whole life outside of the office has just become about when the next time is going to happen, and I'm just so exhausted by it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty after eating a normal meal?\n\nPatient: Guilty after a normal meal? Oh, no, not really. I mean... well, it's different. A normal meal, like... like I mentioned before, if I pack a salad for lunch or have a turkey sandwich at my desk while I'm reviewing the weekly timesheets—because my team has to have their hours submitted by noon on Thursdays or payroll gets entirely backed up, and you wouldn't believe the headache that causes—when I eat something like that, I actually feel good about it. I feel proud, almost. Like I'm finally doing what I'm supposed to be doing. I'll sit there with my little tupperware container and think, 'See? I might feel a little anxious beforehand, worrying about what the menu will look like or if I'll be tempted to order the whole appetizer platter, but the meal itself... if I keep it together, I feel okay. \n\nThe guilt is really tied entirely to the sheer volume of those bad nights. It's the physical evidence of it, the wrappers and the boxes, and the memory of just shoveling it in without even tasting it. That's where the shame comes from. A regular dinner, if I can manage it, actually feels like a tiny little victory, even if it's just for one day. It's just that those victories are getting so incredibly rare lately.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel stressed, anxious, or upset right before one of these episodes of eating a large amount of food?\n\nPatient: Well... yes and no. There has barely been a single week in the last several months without at least one of these nights. And I mean barely. Most weeks... most weeks it happens several times. Like, I'll do it on a Tuesday, and then I'll be so angry with myself on Wednesday that I manage to hold it together, but by Thursday the stress of the office is back up to my neck—maybe someone called out sick or the payroll system glitched—and I'm right back at the drive-thru window. I'll order the food, pull into my driveway, and the whole cycle starts again. \n\nSometimes it's three or four times a week. I try to write down what triggered it, thinking maybe if I can just pinpoint the exact moment my day went wrong, I could fix it. Like, 'Oh, Jane from accounting yelled at me about a missing invoice at 2 PM, so that's why I bought two large pizzas at 6 PM.' But it doesn't even seem to matter anymore. It's just happening all the time, week after week, month after month. It's like my whole life outside of the office has just become about when the next time is going to happen, and I'm just so exhausted by it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty after eating a normal meal?\n\nPatient: Guilty after a normal meal? Oh, no, not really. I mean... well, it's different. A normal meal, like... like I mentioned before, if I pack a salad for lunch or have a turkey sandwich at my desk while I'm reviewing the weekly timesheets—because my team has to have their hours submitted by noon on Thursdays or payroll gets entirely backed up, and you wouldn't believe the headache that causes—when I eat something like that, I actually feel good about it. I feel proud, almost. Like I'm finally doing what I'm supposed to be doing. I'll sit there with my little tupperware container and think, 'See? You can be a normal person who eats a normal amount of food.' \n\nIt's only when the switch flips, you know? When I lose that control. If I go out to a restaurant with a friend—not that I do that very often anymore, because I'm so self-conscious about eating in front of people, but if I do—and I just order a regular entree, maybe a piece of grilled chicken and some vegetables, I don't feel that crushing guilt afterward. I might feel a little anxious beforehand, worrying about what the menu will look like or if I'll be tempted to order the whole appetizer platter, but the meal itself... if I keep it together, I feel okay. \n\nThe guilt is really tied entirely to the sheer volume of those bad nights. It's the physical evidence of it, the wrappers and the boxes, and the memory of just shoveling it in without even tasting it. That's where the shame comes from. A regular dinner, if I can manage it, actually feels like a tiny little victory, even if it's just for one day. It's just that those victories are getting so incredibly rare lately.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel stressed, anxious, or upset right before one of these episodes of eating a large amount of food?\n\nPatient: Well... yes and no. I mean, it's not always this huge, dramatic thing right beforehand. Sometimes it is, of course. Like I was saying about the office—if someone calls out sick and I have to cover their desk on top of managing the whole administrative team, or if the quarterly reports are due and the system crashes... yes, I feel this intense, buzzing sort of stress right behind my eyes. I'll be sitting at my desk, my heart will be racing, and I can almost feel this tightness in my chest, and all I can think about is getting out of there and getting to the food. It's like a pressure valve that needs to be released. In those moments, I am absolutely, undeniably stressed and upset. \n\nBut then... there are other times. Times where I wouldn't necessarily say I'm feeling actively upset or anxious right in that exact moment. Like, it might just be a regular Tuesday evening. I've left the office, the drive home was fine, traffic wasn't even that bad—which is a miracle on the interstate these days—and I'll pull into my driveway, and it's just... quiet. The house is quiet. And it's almost like the absence of stress becomes its own trigger, if that makes sense? It's like this creeping emptiness or boredom, or maybe it's just habit at this point. I'll walk through the front door, put my keys on the little table in the hall, and before I've even taken off my coat, I'm already thinking about what I can order or what's in the pantry. \n\nSo, I guess what I'm trying to say is, I don't always feel this acute, sharp anxiety right before I start eating. Sometimes I do, absolutely, especially when work is chaotic. But other times, it's more like this dull, heavy feeling... like a fog that just rolls in, and the only way I know how to navigate through it is by eating. It's almost like my brain just goes on autopilot, regardless of whether I'm having a terrible day or just a remarkably average one. Does that make sense? It's just... it's not always a clear-cut reaction to a specific upset, even though I know that's probably what you're looking for. It's just so ingrained in my routine now.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating episodes are usually triggered by a specific emotional state, such as sadness or anxiety?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean... I've really, really tried to figure that exact thing out. I actually—and this might sound a little silly—but I actually tried to start color-coding that journal I told you about. Because, you know, in my line of work, if you want to solve a problem with the administrative workflow, you track the metrics. You find the root cause. So I thought, okay, let's track my emotions. I bought these little highlighters, thinking I'd use yellow for anxiety, blue for sadness, maybe pink for anger... just to see if a pattern emerged before I ended up with all those food wrappers. \n\nBut... I honestly don't know. I really couldn't tell you if it's one specific thing like sadness or anxiety. It's just not that clear-cut. I mean, sure, sometimes I think I'm sad. Like, my daughter moved a few states away a couple of years ago, and that was terribly sad, but the nights I missed her the absolute most weren't necessarily the nights I lost control with food. And anxiety... well, like I said before about the payroll glitches and the office, that definitely revs me up. \n\nBut then there are those other days. I'll just be sitting in my living room on a Sunday afternoon, watching one of those home renovation shows on TV, not feeling sad, not feeling anxious, not feeling much of anything at all, really. Just perfectly neutral. And then, out of nowhere, it's like a switch flips and I'm ordering forty dollars worth of Chinese takeout. \n\nSo, I guess... no, I don't think it's usually triggered by a specific emotional state. Or if it is, I certainly haven't been able to identify it. It's so incredibly frustrating, because if I could just say, 'Oh, I eat when I'm sad,' then maybe I could just... I don't know, call a friend or watch a comedy when I feel the sadness coming on. But because it happens when I'm anxious, and when I'm stressed, and when I'm completely fine and just folding laundry... I just don't have a clear answer for you. It's just this big, confusing jumble, and the highlighters didn't help at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have control over what you're eating or the amount you're eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Control? Oh, heavens, no. No, absolutely not. That is... honestly, Doctor, that is the most terrifying part of this whole ordeal. If I had control, I wouldn't be sitting here, you know? Because in literally every other aspect of my life, I am the one in control. I manage the whole front office, I tell people where to be and what to do, I fix the schedules when they completely fall apart, I handle the disputes... I am in charge. But with this? \n\nIt's like... well, I mentioned that switch flipping earlier, but during the actual episode, it's so much worse than just a general feeling. It's a complete disconnect. Once I start, there is this awful switch that flips where I am just... severed from my own body, almost. I can literally be standing there at the kitchen counter, looking at all the food I've laid out, and in my head, my rational brain is still there. I can be thinking, Taylor, put it away. Just close the box, put it in the fridge, walk away. I hear it so clearly in my mind, just begging myself to stop. \n\nBut my hands... they just keep moving. My hands still keep opening containers, ripping off wrappers, pulling out more food, like I am not the one in charge. It is the most bizarre, helpless experience you can imagine. It's like I'm a passenger in my own body, just watching my own hands do these things, and I'm pleading with myself to just put the fork down or close the cabinet door, but I just can't. The physical motion just completely overrides my brain. I'll just keep going and going, mechanically, until it's all gone, all while that voice inside is just screaming to stop. So, no, there is zero control. None whatsoever once that first bite happens. It's like I'm entirely hijacked.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, good heavens, no. No, absolutely not. I mean, the mere thought of someone being there and actually seeing me... it makes my stomach drop just thinking about it. I actually go out of my way—I make a very specific point—to ensure I am completely, one-hundred-percent alone before any of this starts. 

My husband, bless him, he goes to bed quite early, and lately we’ve had these contractors working on the downstairs bathroom and the mudroom. You know how these home renovation projects are, they tell you it’ll take three weeks and suddenly it’s month four and there’s dust everywhere and strangers in your house until all hours. But I will literally just sit there, making small talk or pretending to read a magazine, just waiting. I will wait until I know for an absolute fact that my husband is dead asleep upstairs, or until I hear those contractors finally pack up their tools and their trucks pull out of the driveway. I'll even check the window just to make sure they're really gone.

If they were still there, or if my husband was awake and in the living room... no, I wouldn't do it. Because I would be absolutely mortified. I mean, completely, deeply humiliated for anyone to see the sheer amount of food I can put away in one sitting. It's just so shameful. Especially because... well, like I was telling you about my job, I am the one in charge. I spend eight, nine, sometimes ten hours a day acting so incredibly controlled, managing everyone else's problems, keeping the whole administrative team running smoothly without missing a beat. I have this reputation for being so put-together and disciplined. If anyone—my husband, the guys fixing my drywall, anyone—saw what I do in that kitchen when I'm alone, the way I just lose all that control... they wouldn't even recognize me. They would think I had lost my mind. So, yes, I have to hide it. I make absolutely sure no one is around.

It's like I just blank out or something, and then I'm sitting there with all these empty containers, feeling so sick and so ashamed. I just... My mother had to stay home from work to take care of me, and I just remember feeling so utterly helpless and miserable. So, no, the thought of making myself sick on purpose... it just repulses me, honestly. \n\nAnd as for laxatives, well, I tried them once years ago when I was actually just having some normal digestive issues, and the cramping was so horrendous I swore I'd never touch them again. I remember I was at work, trying to run a staff meeting about the new filing system, and I had to keep excusing myself. It was so embarrassing. \n\nSo, no, I just... I just sit there with it. I sit there with all that food inside me, feeling so incredibly full and bloated and uncomfortable. Sometimes I'll try to drink some peppermint tea or maybe lay on my left side, because I read somewhere once that laying on your left side helps with digestion, but mostly I just suffer through it. I just let it sit there like a rock, a heavy, awful rock, and I just deal with the physical discomfort on top of all the emotional misery I was telling you about. It's like I feel I deserve the physical pain, you know? Like it's my punishment for losing control again. So, no, I don't try to get rid of it. I just let it stay there and make me miserable until it finally passes on its own, which usually takes well into the next day.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating a large amount of food in a short time happen?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... it's a lot. More than I want to admit, honestly. I actually keep a journal—have for years, it's this little leather-bound thing I keep in my nightstand. I started it just to track my daily tasks at work, you know, because keeping track of all the administrative staff's schedules and the endless office supplies orders can get so overwhelming, but lately it's turned into this awful log of my failures. \n\nWhen I look back through it, just flipping through the pages from the last few months... it's really distressing. There has barely been a single week in the last several months without at least one of these nights. And I mean barely. Most weeks... most weeks it happens several times. Like, I'll do it on a Tuesday, and then I'll be so angry with myself on Wednesday that I manage to hold it together, but by Thursday the stress of the office is back up to my neck—maybe someone called out sick or the payroll system glitched—and I'm right back at the drive-thru window. I'll order the food, pull into my driveway, and the whole cycle starts again. \n\nSometimes it's three or four times a week. I try to write down what triggered it, thinking maybe if I can just pinpoint the exact moment my day went wrong, I could fix it. Like, 'Oh, Jane from accounting yelled at me about a missing invoice at 2 PM, so that's why I bought two large pizzas at 6 PM.' But it doesn't even seem to matter anymore. It's just happening all the time, week after week, month after month. It's like my whole life outside of the office has just become about when the next time is going to happen, and I'm just so exhausted by it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty after eating a normal meal?\n\nPatient: Guilty after a normal meal? Oh, no, not really. I mean... well, it's different. A normal meal, like... like I mentioned before, if I pack a salad for lunch or have a turkey sandwich at my desk while I'm reviewing the weekly timesheets—because my team has to have their hours submitted by noon on Thursdays or payroll gets entirely backed up, and you wouldn't believe the headache that causes—when I eat something like that, I actually feel good about it. I feel proud, almost. Like I'm finally doing what I'm supposed to be doing. I'll sit there with my little tupperware container and think, 'See? You can be a normal person who eats a normal amount of food.' \n\nIt's only when the switch flips, you know? When I lose that control. If I go out to a restaurant with a friend—not that I do that very often anymore, because I'm so self-conscious about eating in front of people, but if I do—and I just order a regular entree, maybe a piece of grilled chicken and some vegetables, I don't feel that crushing guilt afterward. I might feel a little anxious beforehand, worrying about what the menu will look like or if I'll be tempted to order the whole appetizer platter, but the meal itself... if I keep it together, I feel okay. \n\nThe guilt is really tied entirely to the sheer volume of those bad nights. It's the physical evidence of it, the wrappers and the boxes, and the memory of just shoveling it in without even tasting it. That's where the shame comes from. A regular dinner, if I can manage it, actually feels like a tiny little victory, even if it's just for one day. It's just that those victories are getting so incredibly rare lately.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel stressed, anxious, or upset right before one of these episodes of eating a large amount of food?\n\nPatient: Well... yes and no. I mean, it's not always this huge, dramatic thing right beforehand. Sometimes it is, of course. Like I was saying about the office—if someone calls out sick and I have to cover their desk on top of managing the whole administrative team, or if the quarterly reports are due and the system crashes... yes, I feel this intense, buzzing sort of stress right behind my eyes. I'll be sitting at my desk, my heart will be racing, and I can almost feel this tightness in my chest, and all I can think about is getting out of there and getting to the food. It's like a pressure valve that needs to be released. In those moments, I am absolutely, undeniably stressed and upset. \n\nBut then... there are other times. Times where I wouldn't necessarily say I'm feeling actively upset or anxious right in that exact moment. Like, it might just be a regular Tuesday evening. I've left the office, the drive home was fine, traffic wasn't even that bad—which is a miracle on the interstate these days—and I'll pull into my driveway, and it's just... quiet. The house is quiet. And it's almost like the absence of stress becomes its own trigger, if that makes sense? It's like this creeping emptiness or boredom, or maybe it's just habit at this point. I'll walk through the front door, put my keys on the little table in the hall, and before I've even taken off my coat, I'm already thinking about what I can order or what's in the pantry. \n\nSo, I guess what I'm trying to say is, I don't always feel this acute, sharp anxiety right before I start eating. Sometimes I do, absolutely, especially when work is chaotic. But other times, it's more like this dull, heavy feeling... like a fog that just rolls in, and the only way I know how to navigate through it is by eating. It's almost like my brain just goes on autopilot, regardless of whether I'm having a terrible day or just a remarkably average one. Does that make sense? It's just... it's not always a clear-cut reaction to a specific upset, even though I know that's probably what you're looking for. It's just so ingrained in my routine now.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating episodes are usually triggered by a specific emotional state, such as sadness or anxiety?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean... I've really, really tried to figure that exact thing out. I actually—and this might sound a little silly—but I actually tried to start color-coding that journal I told you about. Because, you know, in my line of work, if you want to solve a problem with the administrative workflow, you track the metrics. You find the root cause. So I thought, okay, let's track my emotions. I bought these little highlighters, thinking I'd use yellow for anxiety, blue for sadness, maybe pink for anger... just to see if a pattern emerged before I ended up with all those food wrappers. \n\nBut... I honestly don't know. I really couldn't tell you if it's one specific thing like sadness or anxiety. It's just not that clear-cut. I mean, sure, sometimes I think I'm sad. Like, my daughter moved a few states away a couple of years ago, and that was terribly sad, but the nights I missed her the absolute most weren't necessarily the nights I lost control with food. And anxiety... well, like I said before about the payroll glitches and the office, that definitely revs me up. \n\nBut then there are those other days. I'll just be sitting in my living room on a Sunday afternoon, watching one of those home renovation shows on TV, not feeling sad, not feeling anxious, not feeling much of anything at all, really. Just perfectly neutral. And then, out of nowhere, it's like a switch flips and I'm ordering forty dollars worth of Chinese takeout. \n\nSo, I guess... no, I don't think it's usually triggered by a specific emotional state. Or if it is, I certainly haven't been able to identify it. It's so incredibly frustrating, because if I could just say, 'Oh, I eat when I'm sad,' then maybe I could just... I don't know, call a friend or watch a comedy when I feel the sadness coming on. But because it happens when I'm anxious, and when I'm stressed, and when I'm completely fine and just folding laundry... I just don't have a clear answer for you. It's just this big, confusing jumble, and the highlighters didn't help at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have control over what you're eating or the amount you're eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Control? Oh, heavens, no. No, absolutely not. That is... honestly, Doctor, that is the most terrifying part of this whole ordeal. If I had control, I wouldn't be sitting here, you know? Because in literally every other aspect of my life, I am the one in control. I manage the whole front office, I tell people where to be and what to do, I fix the schedules when they completely fall apart, I handle the disputes... I am in charge. But with this? \n\nIt's like... well, I mentioned that switch flipping earlier, but during the actual episode, it's so much worse than just a general feeling. It's a complete disconnect. Once I start, there is this awful switch that flips where I am just... severed from my own body, almost. I can literally be standing there at the kitchen counter, looking at all the food I've laid out, and in my head, my rational brain is still there. I can be thinking, Taylor, put it away. Just close the box, put it in the fridge, walk away. I hear it so clearly in my mind, just begging myself to stop. \n\nBut my hands... they just keep moving. My hands still keep opening containers, ripping off wrappers, pulling out more food, like I am not the one in charge. It is the most bizarre, helpless experience you can imagine. It's like I'm a passenger in my own body, just watching my own hands do these things, and I'm pleading with myself to just put the fork down or close the cabinet door, but I just can't. The physical motion just completely overrides my brain. I'll just keep going and going, mechanically, until it's all gone, all while that voice inside is just screaming to stop. So, no, there is zero control. None whatsoever once that first bite happens. It's like I'm entirely hijacked.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still eat this much if you weren't alone when you got home from work?\n\nPatient: Oh, good heavens, no. No, absolutely not. I mean, the mere thought of someone being there and actually seeing me... it makes my stomach drop just thinking about it. I actually go out of my way—I make a very specific point—to ensure I am completely, one-hundred-percent alone before any of this starts. \n\nMy husband, bless him, he goes to bed quite early, and lately we’ve had these contractors working on the downstairs bathroom and the mudroom. You know how these home renovation projects are, they tell you it’ll take three weeks and suddenly it’s month four and there’s dust everywhere and strangers in your house until all hours. But I will literally just sit there, making small talk or pretending to read a magazine, just waiting.
