I sit there with all that food inside me, feeling so incredibly full and bloated and uncomfortable. Sometimes I'll try to drink some peppermint tea or maybe lay on my left side, because I read somewhere once that laying on your left side helps with digestion, but mostly I just suffer through it. I just let it sit there like a rock, a heavy, awful rock, and I just deal with the physical discomfort on top of all the emotional misery I was telling you about. It's like I feel I deserve the physical pain, you know? Like it's my punishment for losing control again. So, no, I don't try to get rid of it. I just let it stay there and make me miserable until it finally passes on its own, which usually takes well into the next day.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating a large amount of food in a short time happen?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... it's a lot. More than I want to admit, honestly. I actually keep a journal—have for years, it's this little leather-bound thing I keep in my nightstand. I started it just to track my daily tasks at work, you know, because keeping track of all the administrative staff's schedules and the endless office supplies orders can get so overwhelming, but lately it's turned into this awful log of my failures. \n\nWhen I look back through it, just flipping through the pages from the last few months... it's really distressing. There has barely been a single week in the last several months without at least one of these nights. And I mean barely. Most weeks... most weeks it happens several times. Like, I'll do it on a Tuesday, and then I'll be so angry with myself on Wednesday that I manage to hold it together, but by Thursday the stress of the office is back up to my neck—maybe someone called out sick or the payroll system glitched—and I'm right back at the drive-thru window. I'll order the food, pull into my driveway, and the whole cycle starts again. \n\nSometimes it's three or four times a week. I try to write down what triggered it, thinking maybe if I can just pinpoint the exact moment my day went wrong, I could fix it. Like, 'Oh, Jane from accounting yelled at me about a missing invoice at 2 PM, so that's why I bought two large pizzas at 6 PM.' But it doesn't even seem to matter anymore. It's just happening all the time, week after week, month after month. It's like my whole life outside of the office has just become about when the next time is going to happen, and I'm just so exhausted by it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty after eating a normal meal?\n\nPatient: Guilty after a normal meal? Oh, no, not really. I mean... well, it's different. A normal meal, like... like I mentioned before, if I pack a salad for lunch or have a turkey sandwich at my desk while I'm reviewing the weekly timesheets—because my team has to have their hours submitted by noon on Thursdays or payroll gets entirely backed up, and you wouldn't believe the headache that causes—when I eat something like that, I actually feel good about it. I feel proud, almost. Like I'm finally doing what I'm supposed to be doing. I'll sit there with my little tupperware container and think, 'See? You can be a normal person who eats a normal amount of food.' \n\nIt's only when the switch flips, you know? When I lose that control. If I go out to a restaurant with a friend—not that I do that very often anymore, because I'm so self-conscious about eating in front of people, but if I do—and I just order a regular entree, maybe a piece of grilled chicken and some vegetables, I don't feel that crushing guilt afterward. I might feel a little anxious beforehand, worrying about what the menu will look like or if I'll be tempted to order the whole appetizer platter, but the meal itself... if I keep it together, I feel okay. \n\nThe guilt is really tied entirely to the sheer volume of those bad nights. It's the physical evidence of it, the wrappers and the boxes, and the memory of just shoveling it in without even tasting it. That's where the shame comes from. A regular dinner, if I can manage it, actually feels like a tiny little victory, even if it's just for one day. It's just that those victories are getting so incredibly rare lately.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel stressed, anxious, or upset right before one of these episodes of eating a large amount of food?\n\nPatient: Well... yes and no. I mean, it's not always this huge, dramatic thing right beforehand. Sometimes it is, of course. Like I was saying about the office—if someone calls out sick and I have to cover their desk on top of managing the whole administrative team, or if the quarterly reports are due and the system crashes... yes, I feel this intense, buzzing sort of stress right behind my eyes. I'll be sitting at my desk, my heart will be racing, and I can almost feel this tightness in my chest, and all I can think about is getting out of there and getting to the food. It's like a pressure valve that needs to be released. In those moments, I am absolutely, undeniably stressed and upset. \n\nBut then... there are other times. Times where I wouldn't necessarily say I'm feeling actively upset or anxious right in that exact moment. Like, it might just be a regular Tuesday evening. I've left the office, the drive home was fine, traffic wasn't even that bad—which is a miracle on the interstate these days—and I'll pull into my driveway, and it's just... quiet. The house is quiet. And it's almost like the absence of stress becomes its own trigger, if that makes sense? It's like this creeping emptiness or boredom, or maybe it's just habit at this point. I'll walk through the front door, put my keys on the little table in the hall, and before I've even taken off my coat, I'm already thinking about what I can order or what's in the pantry. \n\nSo, I guess what I'm trying to say is, I don't always feel this acute, sharp anxiety right before I start eating. Sometimes I do, absolutely, especially when work is chaotic. But other times, it's more like this dull, heavy feeling... like a fog that just rolls in, and the only way I know how to navigate through it is by eating. It's almost like my brain just goes on autopilot, regardless of whether I'm having a terrible day or just a remarkably average one. Does that make sense? It's just... it's not always a clear-cut reaction to a specific upset, even though I know that's probably what you're looking for. It's just so ingrained in my routine now.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating episodes are usually triggered by a specific emotional state, such as sadness or anxiety?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean... I've really, really tried to figure that exact thing out. I actually—and this might sound a little silly—but I actually tried to start color-coding that journal I told you about. Because, you know, in my line of work, if you want to solve a problem with the administrative workflow, you track the metrics. You find the root cause. So I thought, okay, let's track my emotions. I bought these little highlighters, thinking I'd use yellow for anxiety, blue for sadness, maybe pink for anger... just to see if a pattern emerged before I ended up with all those food wrappers. \n\nBut... I honestly don't know. I really couldn't tell you if it's one specific thing like sadness or anxiety. It's just not that clear-cut. I mean, sure, sometimes I think I'm sad. Like, my daughter moved a few states away a couple of years ago, and that was terribly sad, but the nights I missed her the absolute most weren't necessarily the nights I lost control with food. And anxiety... well, like I said before about the payroll glitches and the office, that definitely revs me up. \n\nBut then there are those other days. I'll just be sitting in my living room on a Sunday afternoon, watching one of those home renovation shows on TV, not feeling sad, not feeling anxious, not feeling much of anything at all, really. Just perfectly neutral. And then, out of nowhere, it's like a switch flips and I'm ordering forty dollars worth of Chinese takeout. \n\nSo, I guess... no, I don't think it's usually triggered by a specific emotional state. Or if it is, I certainly haven't been able to identify it. It's so incredibly frustrating, because if I could just say, 'Oh, I eat when I'm sad,' then maybe I could just... I don't know, call a friend or watch a comedy when I feel the sadness coming on. But because it happens when I'm anxious, and when I'm stressed, and when I'm completely fine and just folding laundry... I just don't have a clear answer for you. It's just this big, confusing jumble, and the highlighters didn't help at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have control over what you're eating or the amount you're eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Control? Oh, heavens, no. No, absolutely not. That is... honestly, Doctor, that is the most terrifying part of this whole ordeal. If I had control, I wouldn't be sitting here, you know? Because in literally every other aspect of my life, I am the one in control. I manage the whole front office, I tell people where to be and what to do, I fix the schedules when they completely fall apart, I handle the disputes... I am in charge. But with this? \n\nIt's like... well, I mentioned that switch flipping earlier, but during the actual episode, it's so much worse than just a general feeling. It's a complete disconnect. Once I start, there is this awful switch that flips where I am just... severed from my own body, almost. I can literally be standing there at the kitchen counter, looking at all the food I've laid out, and in my head, my rational brain is still there. I can be thinking, Taylor, put it away. Just close the box, put it in the fridge, walk away. I hear it so clearly in my mind, just begging myself to stop. \n\nBut my hands... they just keep moving. My hands still keep opening containers, ripping off wrappers, pulling out more food, like I am not the one in charge. It is the most bizarre, helpless experience you can imagine. It's like I'm a passenger in my own body, just watching my own hands do these things, and I'm pleading with myself to just put the fork down or close the cabinet door, but I just can't. The physical motion just completely overrides my brain. I'll just keep going and going, mechanically, until it's all gone, all while that voice inside is just screaming to stop. So, no, there is zero control. None whatsoever once that first bite happens. It's like I'm entirely hijacked.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still eat this much if you weren't alone when you got home from work?\n\nPatient: Oh, good heavens, no. No, absolutely not. I mean, the mere thought of someone being there and actually seeing me... it makes my stomach drop just thinking about it. I actually go out of my way—I make a very specific point—to ensure I am completely, one-hundred-percent alone before any of this starts. \n\nMy husband, bless him, he goes to bed quite early, and lately we’ve had these contractors working on the downstairs bathroom and the mudroom. You know how these home renovation projects are, they tell you it’ll take three weeks and suddenly it’s month four and there’s dust everywhere and strangers in your house until all hours. But I will literally just sit there, making small talk or pretending to read a magazine, just waiting. I will wait until I know for an absolute fact that my husband is dead asleep upstairs, or until I hear those contractors finally pack up their tools and their trucks pull out of the driveway. I'll even check the window just to make sure they're really gone.\n\nIf they were still there, or if my husband was awake and in the living room... no, I wouldn't do it. Because I would be absolutely mortified. I mean, completely, deeply humiliated for anyone to see the sheer amount of food I can put away in one sitting. It's just so shameful. Especially because... well, like I was telling you about my job, I am the one in charge. I spend eight, nine, sometimes ten hours a day acting so incredibly controlled, managing everyone else's problems, keeping the whole administrative team running smoothly without missing a beat. I have this reputation for being so put-together and disciplined. If anyone—my husband, the guys fixing my drywall, anyone—saw what I do in that kitchen when I'm alone, the way I just lose all that control... they wouldn't even recognize me. They would think I had lost my mind. So, yes, I have to hide it. I make absolutely sure no one is around.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating episodes are usually triggered by a specific emotional state, such as feeling overwhelmed or stressed?\n\nPatient: Well, Doctor, it really just goes right back to what I was trying to explain a few minutes ago when we were talking about sadness and anxiety. It’s the exact same thing with being overwhelmed or stressed. I mean, yes, do I get stressed? Absolutely. You try managing a front office where the phone never stops ringing, half the staff is out sick, and the new billing software crashes every single Tuesday at two o'clock. It is the very definition of overwhelming. And sure, sometimes I come home from a day like that, completely frazzled, and the first thing I do is head straight for the pantry. \n\nBut is stress the specific trigger? Is it the one thing that causes this? No, I really don't think so. Because, just like I was saying about those Sunday afternoons watching renovation shows, I can have a completely stress-free day. I can have a day where everyone actually does their jobs, the contractors downstairs don't make a peep, my husband and I have a lovely, quiet morning... a day where I am not overwhelmed in the slightest. And I will still end up standing at that kitchen counter, completely disconnected, eating everything in sight. \n\nIt drives me absolutely up the wall, it really does. Because I am a problem solver. That is what I do for a living. If I knew for a fact that stress was the trigger, I would just fix it. I’d find a way to mitigate the stress. I'd take up yoga, or I'd go for a walk, or I'd finally march into my boss's office and demand an assistant to handle the overflow. I’d have an action plan. But because there is no logical pattern—because it happens when I'm stressed to the max, and it happens when I haven't got a care in the world—I just can't fix it. So, no, I honestly can't blame it on feeling overwhelmed or stressed, as much as I wish I could just point a finger at that and be done with it. It's just as much of a mystery as everything else.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel unfulfilled or dissatisfied with your life in general, outside of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Unfulfilled? Dissatisfied? Oh, no, not at all. That's... well, that's another thing that makes this whole situation so incredibly frustrating. If I hated my job, or if my marriage was falling apart, or if I had some terrible rift with my family, maybe I could look at that and say, \"Aha! That's the problem. That's why I'm doing this.\" But honestly, Doctor, I have a wonderful life. I really do. \n\nI mean, I've been with the same company for over twenty years. I started as a receptionist and worked my way up to managing the entire front office. I have a great team—sure, they drive me crazy sometimes, like I mentioned with the software crashes and the scheduling nightmares, but I genuinely love what I do. I take pride in it. I like being the person people come to when they need something fixed. It gives me a real sense of purpose. \n\nAnd my husband, well, we've been married for almost thirty years now. He is just the sweetest, most patient man. Even with this whole mudroom renovation dragging on forever, he just rolls with the punches. We have a lovely home, we take nice vacations... we just got back from a cruise to Alaska last fall, actually, which was absolutely breathtaking. I highly recommend it if you've never been. The glaciers are just... well, they're magnificent. \n\nSo, no, I don't feel unfulfilled. I don't feel like there's this big, gaping hole in my life that I'm trying to fill with food. I'm not lonely, I'm not depressed about where I ended up... I'm actually very content with my life, outside of this one, horrible, uncontrollable thing. It's like I have this beautiful, perfectly organized house, and there's just this one room in the back that's on fire, and I can't put it out, and I don't even know how the fire started. But the rest of the house? The rest of the house is perfectly fine. I just... I don't understand it. I really don't.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your relationship with your husband or your role as a mother to your daughter is connected to your eating episodes in any way?\n\nPatient: Oh, my husband? No, no, absolutely not. Like I was just saying, he is an absolute saint. I mean, if anything, he is the calm in my storm. He is so steady and supportive, and we really don't even argue that much, aside from the usual bickering about him leaving his shoes right in the middle of the hallway where I trip over them, or whose turn it is to deal with the contractor about the drywall dust. But that's just normal marriage stuff, isn't it? It certainly isn't anything that would drive me to... to do what I do in the kitchen. \n\nAnd my daughter... well, she's twenty-six now, living out in Denver. She works in marketing, doing wonderfully for herself, and we talk on the phone at least twice a week. Usually on my drive home from the office, actually, because with the time difference, it just works out perfectly for her schedule before she gets too deep into her afternoon meetings. We have a beautiful relationship. We really do. I'm so incredibly proud of the woman she's become. \n\nSo, no, I honestly don't see any connection there at all. I mean, I know sometimes people eat because they're unhappy in their marriage, or because their kids are driving them crazy. You hear about that all the time, women hiding in the pantry because their toddlers are screaming or their husbands are neglecting them. But that's just not my life. I don't have those kinds of problems. \n\nIt almost makes me feel worse, in a way? Because I have this incredibly supportive husband who would do anything for me, and a daughter who is just thriving. I have this wonderful family unit. There's no trauma there, no big family drama that I'm trying to swallow down with all this food. It really just circles right back to what I was saying a minute ago, about my life being like a perfectly organized house with just this one room on fire. My family is the solid foundation of that house. They aren't the ones holding the matches, if that makes sense. I just... I wish I could give you a better answer, or a neat little reason like a fight with my husband, but it's definitely not them. It's just me.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over time due to these episodes of eating large amounts of food?\n\nPatient: Significant changes in my weight? You know, you would really, truly think so, wouldn't you? With the sheer volume of... well, with everything I'm consuming when I get into one of those zones, you'd expect me to be buying a whole new wardrobe every six months. But honestly? No. Not really. \n\nI mean, my weight has been almost exactly the same for... gosh, it has to be at least ten or twelve years now. I have these tailored navy slacks that I bought for a big corporate presentation back in, oh, maybe 2015, and I still wear them to the office at least once a week. They still zip up just fine. Sure, maybe they feel a tiny bit snug on a Monday morning if I've had a really bad Sunday afternoon in the pantry, but by Wednesday, they fit perfectly again. \n\nIt is actually completely baffling to me. I don't understand how a person can stand at a kitchen island and put away an entire sleeve of cookies, half a loaf of bread, and whatever else I can get my hands on, and not weigh three hundred pounds. I really don't. Maybe I'm just burning it off because I never sit down at work—I am constantly pacing between the front desk and the back filing room, putting out fires and tracking down missing invoices—but the scale at my annual physical never really moves. It's always right around the exact same number. \n\nWhich... well, I know I should be grateful for that, but sometimes I think it's almost a curse. Because if I were gaining all this weight, if my clothes stopped fitting and everyone could see what I was doing to myself, maybe it would be the wake-up call I need. It would be a visible problem that I'd be forced to solve. But because I look exactly the same, because there are no significant changes to point to... it just stays my little secret. It stays hidden. So, no, the weight hasn't really changed at all, as strange as that sounds.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever tried to follow a specific diet or restrict your food intake in an attempt to control your eating?\n\nPatient: Diet? Oh, goodness, no. No, I've never really been one of those people. \n\nYou know, working in an office setting for as long as I have, especially managing a front office with mostly women, you really see it all. It feels like every single month someone is on a new kick. One week they're all doing Keto, the next it's Paleo, or they're doing that thing where they only eat between noon and eight p.m... what is that called? Intermittent fasting, yes, that's the one. I have one receptionist, bless her heart, who brings in these tiny little glass containers with precisely weighed portions of boiled chicken and steamed broccoli, and she absolutely refuses to touch a single carbohydrate. And whenever we have a birthday or a retirement party—like just last week, we had this beautiful, huge sheet cake for Brenda in accounting who was retiring after thirty years—half the staff won't even look at it because it's not on their \"plan.\" \n\nBut I've just never been like that. I don't restrict myself during the day. I eat a perfectly normal breakfast—usually just some oatmeal or a piece of whole-wheat toast with my coffee while I'm looking over the morning schedule before I head out the door. And for lunch, I'll just have a regular turkey sandwich or a salad from the little deli across the street from my building. And I always, always cook a nice, balanced dinner for me and my husband. We'll have a pork chop, some green beans, maybe a little mashed potato or rice. \n\nSo, I'm not starving myself, if that's what you're wondering. I'm not trying to control these... these moments in the kitchen by eating less the rest of the time or punishing myself with a diet. I've never tried to put myself on some strict regimen to fix it, because, honestly, I don't think a diet is the issue. Like I was just saying, my weight is perfectly fine. It's not about me being ravenously hungry because I skipped lunch or denied myself a piece of bread at dinner. It's just... it's something else entirely that takes over. So, no, no specific diets or restricting my food at all. I just eat like a completely normal person, right up until the moment I don't.\n\nDoctor: Do you often criticize yourself after one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Criticize myself? You mean like... beating myself up about it, or calling myself names? Honestly, no. I don't really do that. \n\nI know that's probably what you'd expect, because you see it on television all the time, right? The woman sitting on the kitchen floor, surrounded by empty boxes, just sobbing and telling herself how awful and out of control she is. But that's just not me. \n\nWhen the whole thing is over, when I finally stop, it's almost like a switch just flips back to normal. I don't sit there wallowing in it or analyzing my flaws. I just go straight into, well, I guess you could call it manager mode. It’s exactly how I handle things at the office. If one of the girls at the front desk completely botches a massive supply order or messes up the scheduling software, I don't stand around agonizing over it or berating myself for not checking her work. I just pull up the purchase orders, make the phone calls, and fix the mess. \n\nSo that's what I do in my kitchen. I just gather up all the wrappers and empty packages, I tie up the bag, and I take the trash right out to the big bin in the garage. Then I clean. I wipe down the granite countertops—I always use this Mrs. Meyer's lemon spray, it's just wonderful, it cuts right through any crumbs or stickiness and makes the whole room smell fresh and clean again—and I put whatever is left back exactly where it belongs in the pantry. I make sure the kitchen looks absolutely perfect, like a magazine cover. \n\nAnd then I just go upstairs, wash my face, and go to bed or go fold laundry. I don't sit there thinking, 'Oh, you're so weak, you're so stupid.' I don't have this running inner monologue of criticism. I just feel sort of... blank, I guess? Just quiet and tired. I just want to reset the room and pretend the whole thing never even happened. So, no, I don't really criticize myself at all. I just clean up the mess and move on to the next task.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Vomit? Oh, heavens, no. No, absolutely not. I mean, the very thought of that... no. 

I know that's something people do, especially young girls. You hear about it on the news or read about it in those magazines at the grocery checkout, where they talk about celebrities doing all sorts of drastic things to stay thin. But for me? Never. Like I was saying just a moment ago about how I handle the aftermath—cleaning up the kitchen, wiping down the counters, putting everything back in its place—throwing up would just be... well, it would be a whole other ordeal, wouldn't it? It's messy, it's unpleasant, and frankly, it just seems so undignified. 

I actually despise being sick to my stomach. Even when I catch a regular stomach bug, which, thankfully, is incredibly rare for me... maybe once every five or six years? Usually it happens when one of the girls at the front desk comes to work when she shouldn't have and spreads it around the whole office. But even then, I just hate the feeling of hanging over a toilet. It’s awful. And it's terrible for your teeth, too! My dentist, Dr. Aris—he’s been my dentist for almost twenty years now—he's always lecturing about enamel and stomach acids and how you have to be so careful. 

So, no, I would never intentionally make myself sick like that. After I finish eating all that food, yes, I feel terribly, terribly full, and sometimes a bit sluggish or heavy, but my instinct is never to try and undo it by getting sick. I just pack the trash away, make sure the house is perfectly tidy, and go upstairs. I just let it digest and try to go to sleep and pretend the whole evening didn't happen. I really just want to put it behind me as quickly as possible, and making myself throw up would just drag the whole miserable experience out even longer. So, no, never that. Not even once.

I'll even check the window just to make sure they're really gone.\n\nIf they were still there, or if my husband was awake and in the living room... no, I wouldn't do it. Because I would be absolutely mortified. I mean, completely, deeply humiliated for anyone to see the sheer amount of food I can put away in one sitting. It's just so shameful. Especially because... well, like I was telling you about my job, I am the one in charge. I spend eight, nine, sometimes ten hours a day acting so incredibly controlled, managing everyone else's problems, keeping the whole administrative team running smoothly without missing a beat. I have this reputation for being so put-together and disciplined. If anyone—my husband, the guys fixing my drywall, anyone—saw what I do in that kitchen when I'm alone, the way I just lose all that control... they wouldn't even recognize me. They would think I had lost my mind. So, yes, I have to hide it. I make absolutely sure no one is around.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating episodes are usually triggered by a specific emotional state, such as feeling overwhelmed or stressed?\n\nPatient: Well, Doctor, it really just goes right back to what I was trying to explain a few minutes ago when we were talking about sadness and anxiety. It’s the exact same thing with being overwhelmed or stressed. I mean, yes, do I get stressed? Absolutely. You try managing a front office where the phone never stops ringing, half the staff is out sick, and the new billing software crashes every single Tuesday at two o'clock. It is the very definition of overwhelming. And sure, sometimes I come home from a day like that, completely frazzled, and the first thing I do is head straight for the pantry. \n\nBut is stress the specific trigger? Is it the one thing that causes this? No, I really don't think so. Because, just like I was saying about those Sunday afternoons watching renovation shows, I can have a completely stress-free day. I can have a day where everyone actually does their jobs, the contractors downstairs don't make a peep, my husband and I have a lovely, quiet morning... a day where I am not overwhelmed in the slightest. And I will still end up standing at that kitchen counter, completely disconnected, eating everything in sight. \n\nIt drives me absolutely up the wall, it really does. Because I am a problem solver. That is what I do for a living. If I knew for a fact that stress was the trigger, I would just fix it. I’d find a way to mitigate the stress. I'd take up yoga, or I'd go for a walk, or I'd finally march into my boss's office and demand an assistant to handle the overflow. I’d have an action plan. But because there is no logical pattern—because it happens when I'm stressed to the max, and it happens when I haven't got a care in the world—I just can't fix it. So, no, I honestly can't blame it on feeling overwhelmed or stressed, as much as I wish I could just point a finger at that and be done with it. It's just as much of a mystery as everything else.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel unfulfilled or dissatisfied with your life in general, outside of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Unfulfilled? Dissatisfied? Oh, no, not at all. That's... well, that's another thing that makes this whole situation so incredibly frustrating. If I hated my job, or if my marriage was falling apart, or if I had some terrible rift with my family, maybe I could look at that and say, \"Aha! That's the problem. That's why I'm doing this.\" But honestly, Doctor, I have a wonderful life. I really do. \n\nI mean, I've been with the same company for over twenty years. I started as a receptionist and worked my way up to managing the entire front office. I like being the person people come to when they need something fixed. It gives me a real sense of purpose. \n\nAnd my husband, well, we've been married for almost thirty years now. He is just the sweetest, most patient man. Even with this whole mudroom renovation dragging on forever, he just rolls with the punches. We have a lovely home, we take nice vacations... we just got back from a cruise to Alaska last fall, actually, which was absolutely breathtaking. I highly recommend it if you've never been. The glaciers are just... well, they're magnificent. \n\nSo, no, I don't feel unfulfilled. I don't feel like there's this big, gaping hole in my life that I'm trying to fill with food. I'm not lonely, I'm not depressed about where I ended up... I'm actually very content with my life, outside of this one, horrible, uncontrollable thing. It's like I have this beautiful, perfectly organized house, and there's just this one room in the back that's on fire, and I can't put it out, and I don't even know how the fire started. But the rest of the house? The rest of the house is perfectly fine. I just... I don't understand it. I really don't.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your relationship with your husband or your role as a mother to your daughter is connected to your eating episodes in any way?\n\nPatient: Oh, my husband? No, no, absolutely not. Like I was just saying, he is an absolute saint. I mean, if anything, he is the calm in my storm. He is so steady and supportive, and we really don't even argue that much, aside from the usual bickering about him leaving his shoes right in the middle of the hallway where I trip over them, or whose turn it is to deal with the contractor about the drywall dust. I can literally be standing there at the kitchen counter, looking at all the food I've laid out, and in my head, my rational brain is still there. I can be thinking, Taylor, put it away. Just close the box, put it in the fridge, walk away. I hear it so clearly in my mind, just begging myself to stop. \n\nBut my hands... they just keep moving. My hands still keep opening containers, ripping off wrappers, pulling out more food, like I am not the one in charge. It is the most bizarre, helpless experience you can imagine. It's like I'm a passenger in my own body, just watching my own hands do these things, and I'm pleading with myself to just put the fork down or close the cabinet door, but I just can't. The physical motion just completely overrides my brain. I'll just keep going and going, mechanically, until it's all gone, all while that voice inside is just screaming to stop. So, no, there is zero control. None whatsoever once that first bite happens. It's like I'm entirely hijacked.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still eat this much if you weren't alone when you got home from work?\n\nPatient: Oh, good heavens, no. No, absolutely not. I mean, the mere thought of someone being there and actually seeing me... it makes my stomach drop just thinking about it. I actually go out of my way—I make a very specific point—to ensure I am completely, one-hundred-percent alone before any of this starts. \n\nMy husband, bless him, he goes to bed quite early, and lately we’ve had these contractors working on the downstairs bathroom and the mudroom. You know how these home renovation projects are, they tell you it’ll take three weeks and suddenly it’s month four and there’s dust everywhere and strangers in your house until all hours. But I will literally just sit there, making small talk or pretending to read a magazine, just waiting. I will wait until I know for an absolute fact that my husband is dead asleep upstairs, or until I hear those contractors finally pack up their tools and their trucks pull out of the driveway. I'll even check the window just to make sure they're really gone.\n\nIf they were still there, or if my husband was awake and in the living room... no, I wouldn't do it. Because I would be absolutely mortified. I mean, completely, deeply humiliated for anyone to see the sheer amount of food I can put away in one sitting. It's just so shameful. Especially because... well, like I was telling you about my job, I am the one in charge. I spend eight, nine, sometimes ten hours a day acting so incredibly controlled, managing everyone else's problems, keeping the whole administrative team running smoothly without missing a beat. I have this reputation for being so put-together and disciplined. If anyone—my husband, the guys fixing my drywall, anyone—saw what I do in that kitchen when I'm alone, the way I just lose all that control... they wouldn't even recognize me. They would think I had lost my mind. So, yes, I have to hide it. I make absolutely sure no one is around.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating episodes are usually triggered by a specific emotional state, such as feeling overwhelmed or stressed?\n\nPatient: Well, Doctor, it really just goes right back to what I was trying to explain a few minutes ago when we were talking about sadness and anxiety. It’s the exact same thing with being overwhelmed or stressed. I mean, yes, do I get stressed? Absolutely. You try managing a front office where the phone never stops ringing, half the staff is out sick, and the new billing software crashes every single Tuesday at two o'clock. It is the very definition of overwhelming. And sure, sometimes I come home from a day like that, completely frazzled, and the first thing I do is head straight for the pantry. \n\nBut is stress the specific trigger? Is it the one thing that causes this? No, I really don't think so. Because, just like I was saying about those Sunday afternoons watching renovation shows, I can have a completely stress-free day. I can have a day where everyone actually does their jobs, the contractors downstairs don't make a peep, my husband and I have a lovely, quiet morning... a day where I am not overwhelmed in the slightest. And I will still end up standing at that kitchen counter, completely disconnected, eating everything in sight. \n\nIt drives me absolutely up the wall, it really does. Because I am a problem solver. That is what I do for a living. If I knew for a fact that stress was the trigger, I would just fix it. I’d find a way to mitigate the stress. I'd take up yoga, or I'd go for a walk, or I'd finally march into my boss's office and demand an assistant to handle the overflow. I’d have an action plan. But because there is no logical pattern—because it happens when I'm stressed to the max, and it happens when I haven't got a care in the world—I just can't fix it. So, no, I honestly can't blame it on feeling overwhelmed or stressed, as much as I wish I could just point a finger at that and be done with it. It's just as much of a mystery as everything else.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel unfulfilled or dissatisfied with your life in general, outside of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Unfulfilled? Dissatisfied? Oh, no, not at all. That's... well, that's another thing that makes this whole situation so incredibly frustrating. If I hated my job, or if my marriage was falling apart, or if I had some terrible rift with my family, maybe I could look at that and say, \"Aha! That's the problem. That's why I'm doing this.\" But honestly, Doctor, I have a wonderful life. I really do. \n\nI mean, I've been with the same company for over twenty years. I started as a receptionist and worked my way up to managing the entire front office. I have a great team—sure, they drive me crazy sometimes, like I mentioned with the software crashes and the scheduling nightmares, but I genuinely love what I do. I take pride in it. I like being the person people come to when they need something fixed. It gives me a real sense of purpose. \n\nAnd my husband, well, we've been married for almost thirty years now. He is just the sweetest, most patient man. Even with this whole mudroom renovation dragging on forever, he just rolls with the punches. We have a lovely home, we take nice vacations... we just got back from a cruise to Alaska last fall, actually, which was absolutely breathtaking. I highly recommend it if you've never been. The glaciers are just... well, they're magnificent. \n\nSo, no, I don't feel unfulfilled. I don't feel like there's this big, gaping hole in my life that I'm trying to fill with food. I'm not lonely, I'm not depressed about where I ended up... I'm actually very content with my life, outside of this one, horrible, uncontrollable thing. It's like I have this beautiful, perfectly organized house, and there's just this one room in the back that's on fire, and I can't put it out, and I don't even know how the fire started. But the rest of the house? The rest of the house is perfectly fine. I just... I don't understand it. I really don't.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your relationship with your husband or your role as a mother to your daughter is connected to your eating episodes in any way?\n\nPatient: Oh, my husband? No, no, absolutely not. Like I was just saying, he is an absolute saint. I mean, if anything, he is the calm in my storm. He is so steady and supportive, and we really don't even argue that much, aside from the usual bickering about him leaving his shoes right in the middle of the hallway where I trip over them, or whose turn it is to deal with the contractor about the drywall dust. But that's just normal marriage stuff, isn't it? It certainly isn't anything that would drive me to... to do what I do in the kitchen. \n\nAnd my daughter... well, she's twenty-six now, living out in Denver. She works in marketing, doing wonderfully for herself, and we talk on the phone at least twice a week. Usually on my drive home from the office, actually, because with the time difference, it just works out perfectly for her schedule before she gets too deep into her afternoon meetings. We have a beautiful relationship. We really do. I'm so incredibly proud of the woman she's become. \n\nSo, no, I honestly don't see any connection there at all. I mean, I know sometimes people eat because they're unhappy in their marriage, or because their kids are driving them crazy. You hear about that all the time, women hiding in the pantry because their toddlers are screaming or their husbands are neglecting them. But that's just not my life. I don't have those kinds of problems. \n\nIt almost makes me feel worse, in a way? Because I have this incredibly supportive husband who would do anything for me, and a daughter who is just thriving. I have this wonderful family unit. There's no trauma there, no big family drama that I'm trying to swallow down with all this food. It really just circles right back to what I was saying a minute ago, about my life being like a perfectly organized house with just this one room on fire. My family is the solid foundation of that house. They aren't the ones holding the matches, if that makes sense. I just... I wish I could give you a better answer, or a neat little reason like a fight with my husband, but it's definitely not them. It's just me.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over time due to these episodes of eating large amounts of food?\n\nPatient: Significant changes in my weight? You know, you would really, truly think so, wouldn't you? With the sheer volume of... well, with everything I'm consuming when I get into one of those zones, you'd expect me to be buying a whole new wardrobe every six months. But honestly? No. Not really. \n\nI mean, my weight has been almost exactly the same for... gosh, it has to be at least ten or twelve years now. I have these tailored navy slacks that I bought for a big corporate presentation back in, oh, maybe 2015, and I still wear them to the office at least once a week. They still zip up just fine. Sure, maybe they feel a tiny bit snug on a Monday morning if I've had a really bad Sunday afternoon in the pantry, but by Wednesday, they fit perfectly again. \n\nIt is actually completely baffling to me. I don't understand how a person can stand at a kitchen island and put away an entire sleeve of cookies, half a loaf of bread, and whatever else I can get my hands on, and not weigh three hundred pounds. I really don't. Maybe I'm just burning it off because I never sit down at work—I am constantly pacing between the front desk and the back filing room, putting out fires and tracking down missing invoices—but the scale at my annual physical never really moves. It's always right around the exact same number. \n\nWhich... well, I know I should be grateful for that, but sometimes I think it's almost a curse. Because if I were gaining all this weight, if my clothes stopped fitting and everyone could see what I was doing to myself, maybe it would be the wake-up call I need. It would be a visible problem that I'd be forced to solve. But because I look exactly the same, because there are no significant changes to point to... it just stays my little secret. It stays hidden. So, no, the weight hasn't really changed at all, as strange as that sounds.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever tried to follow a specific diet or restrict your food intake in an attempt to control your eating?\n\nPatient: Diet? Oh, goodness, no. No, I've never really been one of those people. \n\nYou know, working in an office setting for as long as I have, especially managing a front office with mostly women, you really see it all. It feels like every single month someone is on a new kick. One week they're all doing Keto, the next it's Paleo, or they're doing that thing where they only eat between noon and eight p.m... what is that called? Intermittent fasting, yes, that's the one. I have one receptionist, bless her heart, who brings in these tiny little glass containers with precisely weighed portions of boiled chicken and steamed broccoli, and she absolutely refuses to touch a single carbohydrate. And whenever we have a birthday or a retirement party—like just last week, we had this beautiful, huge sheet cake for Brenda in accounting who was retiring after thirty years—half the staff won't even look at it because it's not on their \"plan.\" \n\nBut I've just never been like that. I don't restrict myself during the day. I eat a perfectly normal breakfast—usually just some oatmeal or a piece of whole-wheat toast with my coffee while I'm looking over the morning schedule before I head out the door. And for lunch, I'll just have a regular turkey sandwich or a salad from the little deli across the street from my building. And I always, always cook a nice, balanced dinner for me and my husband. We'll have a pork chop, some green beans, maybe a little mashed potato or rice. \n\nSo, I'm not starving myself, if that's what you're wondering. I'm not trying to control these... these moments in the kitchen by eating less the rest of the time or punishing myself with a diet. I've never tried to put myself on some strict regimen to fix it, because, honestly, I don't think a diet is the issue. Like I was just saying, my weight is perfectly fine. It's not about me being ravenously hungry because I skipped lunch or denied myself a piece of bread at dinner. It's just... it's something else entirely that takes over. So, no, no specific diets or restricting my food at all. I just eat like a completely normal person, right up until the moment I don't.\n\nDoctor: Do you often criticize yourself after one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Criticize myself? You mean like... beating myself up about it, or calling myself names? Honestly, no. I don't really do that. \n\nI know that's probably what you'd expect, because you see it on television all the time, right? The woman sitting on the kitchen floor, surrounded by empty boxes, just sobbing and telling herself how awful and out of control she is. But that's just not me. \n\nWhen the whole thing is over, when I finally stop, it's almost like a switch just flips back to normal. I don't sit there wallowing in it or analyzing my flaws. I just go straight into, well, I guess you could call it manager mode. It’s exactly how I handle things at the office. If one of the girls at the front desk completely botches a massive supply order or messes up the scheduling software, I don't stand around agonizing over it or berating myself for not checking her work. I just pull up the purchase orders, make the phone calls, and fix the mess. \n\nSo that's what I do in my kitchen. I just gather up all the wrappers and empty packages, I tie up the bag, and I take the trash right out to the big bin in the garage. Then I clean. I wipe down the granite countertops—I always use this Mrs. Meyer's lemon spray, it's just wonderful, it cuts right through any crumbs or stickiness and makes the whole room smell fresh and clean again—and I put whatever is left back exactly where it belongs in the pantry. I make sure the kitchen looks absolutely perfect, like a magazine cover. \n\nAnd then I just go upstairs, wash my face, and go to bed or go fold laundry. I don't sit there thinking, 'Oh, you're so weak, you're so stupid.' I don't have this running inner monologue of criticism. I just feel sort of... blank, I guess? Just quiet and tired. I just want to reset the room and pretend the whole thing never even happened. So, no, I don't really criticize myself at all. I just clean up the mess and move on to the next task.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever made yourself vomit after eating?\n\nPatient: Vomit? Oh, heavens, no. No, absolutely not. I mean, the very thought of that... no. \n\nI know that's something people do, especially young girls. You hear about it on the news or read about it in those magazines at the grocery checkout, where they talk about celebrities doing all sorts of drastic things to stay thin. But for me? Never. Like I was saying just a moment ago about how I handle the aftermath—cleaning up the kitchen, wiping down the counters, putting everything back in its place—throwing up would just be... well, it would be a whole other ordeal, wouldn't it? It's messy, it's unpleasant, and frankly, it just seems so undignified. \n\nI actually despise being sick to my stomach. Even when I catch a regular stomach bug, which, thankfully, is incredibly rare for me... maybe once every five or six years? Usually it happens when one of the girls at the front desk comes to work when she shouldn't have and spreads it around the whole office. But even then, I just hate the feeling of hanging over a toilet. It’s awful. And it's terrible for your teeth, too! My dentist, Dr. Aris—he’s been my dentist for almost twenty years now—he's always lecturing about enamel and stomach acids and how you have to be so careful. \n\nSo, no, I would never intentionally make myself sick like that. After I finish eating all that food, yes, I feel terribly, terribly full, and sometimes a bit sluggish or heavy, but my instinct is never to try and undo it by getting sick. I just pack the trash away, make sure the house is perfectly tidy, and go upstairs. I just let it digest and try to go to sleep and pretend the whole evening didn't happen. I really just want to put it behind me as quickly as possible, and making myself throw up would just drag the whole miserable experience out even longer. So, no, never that. Not even once."
  }
